**Data and alignment.** The nominal hydraulic model generates pressure-training labels. A reconstructor uses sparse pressure at t; a predictor uses tâˆ’T through tâˆ’1 to estimate t. The next-step formulation is therefore another direct temporal antecedent. SCADA 2018 determines diagnostic thresholds and 2019 evaluates detection/localization; sensor-relocation experiments assess generalization.

**Results and version control.** In the local 2024 preprint, Table 3 reports overall relative prediction errors of 0.3774Â±0.1588% for ChebNet, 0.3249Â±0.0685% for AIGNN, 0.2964Â±0.0874% for ChebNetIN and 0.2077Â±0.0074% for ChebNetEMB. These are the paper's norm-based relative errors, not MAE in metres or a persistence comparison at +15 minutes. Reconstruction Table 2, prediction Table 3 and relocation Table 4 must not be mixed. The published 2026 version has additional false-alarm reporting; its figures are not silently merged with the preprint.

**Relation.** The work establishes temporal graph prediction, max-flow-informed transfer and sensitivity to sensor placement. It prevents presenting topology or algorithmic features alone as our novelty. No selected author-code snapshot for AIGNN is claimed in our repository inventory.

[Local preprint source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.02797), PDF pp.15â€“21; [published version](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ress.2025.111494).

## 7. Schaller et al. Â· Liquor-HGNN

**Task/model.** A heterogeneous graph distinguishes monitored/unmonitored nodes and four relation types. NeuralProphet fills demand information; the graph classifier assigns leak probabilities to links using binary cross-entropy. The selected implementation labels the same snapshot as its pressure inputs. It does not regress pressure into the future.

**Protocol/results.** The paper uses 80% of 2018 for training, 20% for validation/threshold selection and 2019 for test. Its Table 1 reports TPR=47.83%, 46 FP and EUR271,584. The conserved 2026 README instead reports 11 TP, 51 FP, 12 FN and EUR282,785.1. These versions remain separate. The discussion explains why optimizing economic score may increase false alarms; no ranking is transferred to our forecasting task.

**Static concerns.** The selected snapshot computes mode-specific scales, including test data. Its `expected_demands.py` reads a 2018 demand filename for the variable named 2019 and fits annual demand models in ways that require an availability audit. These are concerns in that code recipe, not a reproduced demonstration that the 2023 paper's experiment is wrong. Simulated demands/flows are additional resources, not automatically available observed measurements.

[Paper](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-3630/LWDA2023-paper40.pdf), PDF pp.7â€“10; [snapshot](https://github.com/MilanShao/Liquor-HGNN/tree/64ff247902e702d89400eac74f06aac45d2ea292), commit `64ff247902e7`.

## 8. Truong et al. Â· GATRes

**Task/protocol.** Residual graph attention reconstructs the current pressure state from masked current observations. Training snapshots are simulated, with sparse-sensor masks; robustness to sensor positions, mask rates, noise and shifted demand distributions is studied. The benchmark comparison uses generated snapshots split 60/20/20 and a separate demand-pattern evaluation. L-Town's latter test is a one-week simulation at five-minute resolution, not the official measured-pressure forecast in 2019.

**Results depend on the test.** Table 10 reports L-Town MAE=0.9501Â±0.0086 m for GATRes, 1.5928Â±0.0050 m for mGCN and 3.4505Â±0.0129 m for GraphConvWat. On same-generation-distribution snapshots in Table 11, mGCN has MAE=0.2441Â±0.0014 m versus GATRes=0.4930Â±0.0028 m. A model ranking therefore cannot be quoted without its protocol. A five-minute sampling interval does not turn this spatial reconstruction into a five-minute forecast.

**Code/relevance.** The selected training masks `data.x` while comparing with the same snapshot's `data.y`; train statistics are passed to validation. This is a useful preparation reference. Sparse sensors and robustness are established topics; our extension needs a specific prospective forecast and reading-age question.

[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1029/2023WR036741), PDF pp.19â€“21; [snapshot](https://github.com/DiTEC-project/gnn-pressure-estimation/tree/bb287ed2ae091b29fa19f2417406756b57a45a1f), commit `bb287ed2ae09`.

## 9. Other verified families and their scope

| Work | Data and learning target | Contribution/result | Boundary for our proposal |
|---|---|---|---|
| [GSI + dictionary learning](https://doi.org/10.1109/CCTA49430.2022.9966160) | New area-A scenarios: 30 leak nodes, four-day train/three-day test | Virtual head interpolation and multiple dictionaries/voting; reported accuracy up to 96.54% | Leak-location classification, not original 2019 pressure forecasting |
| [Rajabi et al., GAN + SSIM](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2023.120012) | Generated nominal demand/pressure maps and selected leak cases | Conditional GAN, kriging and SSIM; map MAPE 1.42% with 780 points and 0.67% with 33 | Current-condition healthy maps; five consecutive exceedances are alarm persistence, not forecast horizon |
| [Doss et al., AE/VAE/LSTM-AE](https://doi.org/10.2166/ws.2024.174) | Nominal training and 33 separate simulated single-leak scenarios | Reconstructs 144-sample/12-hour sequences and detects changes | Same-sequence reconstruction; different simultaneous-leak mixture from official SCADA |
| [Mobile sensor method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.19900) | Own scenarios plus a p257 case | Iterative placement improves localization with few fixed/mobile sensors | Sensor budget is already studied; relocation adds operational delay |
| [D-UKF-AW-GSI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.11687) | Current-state fusion and localization; some tests add 100 simulated AMR sensors in A | Graph interpolation plus Kalman head/flow estimates | Kalman prediction is not a validated observed-pressure +15-minute benchmark |
| [Topological signal processing](https://www.eurasip.org/Proceedings/Eusipco/Eusipco2025/pdfs/0002407.pdf) | Synthetic flow signals on L-Town topology | Cell-complex diffusion and sparse anomaly inference | Does not use the original measurement coverage as our test |
| [Li and Chen, ensemble](https://doi.org/10.3390/w18172220) | 1,050 generated nighttime scenarios; 840/210 stratified split | Seven-zone RF/XGBoost/MLP stacking, eight selected sensors, SHAP; accuracy/macro-F1â‰ˆ0.9667/0.9666 | Zonal classification; topology, feature selection, ensembles and interpretability already combined |
| [SICAMS](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eswa.2026.131450) | Official SCADA plus additional simulations | Whitening/Mahalanobis, Hotelling TÂ², hysteresis, PELT, sensor attribution and Laplacian prelocalization | Detection/classification/current loss estimate; recalibration already exists |

SICAMS also illustrates deterioration in water-loss regression during the second half of 2019, supporting the need to test regime change. Its sensor-fault examples use synthetic perturbations, not recorded original fault labels. The selected [SIAM code](https://github.com/dorofeev2025/SIAM/tree/7e8970700212229ea6e715274790e6dc9536c03d) is an area-A 2018 demonstration, not the entire paper pipeline. PELT classification and post-event analysis must be distinguished from prospective alarm issuance.

## 10. Pending and auxiliary sources

**AGN (2025)** remains partially verified: its title describes demand-state forecasting, while the consulted formulation/metrics involve hydraulic states and metres. The exact target, horizon and code remain unresolved. It uses a modified L-Town scenario with a chronological 70/20/10 split. It is neither discarded as unrelated nor cited as proof of an identical +15-minute benchmark. [Primary source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911).

**GASTL (2025)** concerns cross-network pressure estimation/transfer under limited sensors. The editorial consultation does not substitute a validated local full PDF or an original-SCADA forecast protocol. [Primary source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2025.124475).

hDA-RNN/hDS-RNN forecasts pressure/flow on another real network, sampled every 30 minutes; its four-step horizon is two hours. WeLL uses other networks, and the centrality-aware study also concerns other networks. They establish external methodological precedents but are not L-Town results. DiTEC generated datasets and EPyT-Flow are infrastructure; GraphConvWat is a reconstruction antecedent. Availability of their repositories is not proof of a reproduced result on our task.

## 11. What can be claimed after this comparison

Pressure prediction, temporal history, topology, sparse sensors, adaptation, ensembles and robustness all have precedents. The reviewed corpus has not established a homogeneous solution/evaluation for our exact **observed-pressure +15-minute official-SCADA task, equal information budgets, strong causal baselines and separately controlled reading age**. This is a gap in the reviewed corpus, not proof of worldwide novelty.

Our contribution still needs a specified rule and experimental evidence. Every comparison must align target, horizon, sensor coverage, data origin, units, chronological boundaries, tuning budget and availability. Leak truth may annotate evaluation regimes, but must not select test-based hyperparameters or enter operational forecast inputs. These conclusions preserve the Milestone 1 objective while recognizing what the literature already contributes.

The official-label audit also found active leaks at every 2019 timestamp. A global â€œany leak/no leakâ€ classifier would therefore lack negative examples in that test. This does not mean that individual pipe labels lack negatives: pipe classification, new-event detection and observed-pressure regression are different targets.

## 12. Traceability and limitations

The [full state of the art](#/miniproject/milestone-2/estado-del-arte), [46-file matrix](#/miniproject/milestone-2/matriz), [repository index](#/miniproject/milestone-2/repositorios) and [static code review](#/miniproject/milestone-2/codigo-y-curso) contain the supporting versions, page references and code evidence. This chapter prioritizes task-relevant detail; it does not turn every snapshot into a complete executable clone or every result into a verified reproduction.

The report uses a shorter synthesis across its two literature pages. This companion remains the detailed research repository, so the five-page limit does not force the evidence and qualifications to disappear.

At five-minute sampling this establishes a **one-step/+5-minute horizon**. Current reconstruction instead takes its label from row iâˆ’1. This code distinction resolves the ambiguity created by treating every learned pressure estimate as a forecast.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Diagnostic evaluation.** Node residuals are predictor minus reconstructor; neighboring node differences create edge residuals. The article uses seven-day moving statistics, a threshold parameter alpha=1, mean correction using identified non-faulty periods and filtering to suppress repeated alarms within a six-hop neighborhood. Its configuration reports 19 TP, 138 FP, four FN and an economic score of EUR326,521. Those are diagnostic/event results, not pressure MAE at +15 minutes. Some application calls omit temporal-mode arguments and inherit reconstruction defaults, creating a static channel/recipe concern. We did not execute the code and do not conclude that every notebook fails. A causal reproduction must explicitly fix modes, scaling, targets and data boundaries.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Relation to our proposal.** This is a direct pressure-prediction antecedent. Extending +5 to +15 minutes and evaluating recorded pressure against persistence are useful changes, but the mere existence of a temporal or topology-aware predictor is already established. Â· SAM-kNN adaptive virtual sensors\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task and data.** The paper constructs ten three-month WNTR scenarios for modified L-Town area A: 661 nodes, 766 links and 29 pressure sensors, sampled every five minutes. Five scenarios contain one leak each; five contain a sensor fault, including drift/overflow, noise, offset, zero readings or a shift. These are additional scenarios, not the complete original 2019 mixture of simultaneous leaks.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Model.** For each pressure sensor, a virtual sensor predicts from the other pressure sensors. SAM-kNN combines short-term, long-term and combined memories, tracks their errors, chooses a memory and cleans/compresses examples to adapt to drift while retaining previous concepts. Predicted-versus-observed discrepancies trigger anomaly alarms. It is more than ordinary fixed-window kNN.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Verified time construction.** The paper describes a four-sample window; the exact selected preprocessing is more precise: with `time_win_len=4`, X averages rows t,t+1,t+2 and Y takes row t+3. Relative to the last input, that is **the next five-minute sample**. The regressor excludes the target sensor's input when creating its virtual sensor. It must not be characterized as merely spatial reconstruction.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluation and results.** The scenarios are streamed in batches of 200. The selected experiment predicts/detects a batch before updating with that batch's labels. The leakage table reports TP=1 and FN=0 for each of the five scenarios, with FP=[48,20,3,20,17]. Crucially, TP/FN indicate whether the single event was detected, whereas every individual false alarm contributes to FP. Those counts cannot be pooled as if they were balanced row-wise classification statistics, and are not pressure forecast MAE.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Limits and relation.** The repository states that its scenario data are not distributed; training was not reproduced here. Adaptation already has a direct antecedent. For an operational +15-minute extension, an update using the label of origin u is permitted only after that target is observed at u+15. Batch order alone does not prove equivalent forecast availability or real-time detection delay.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62), PDF pp.8â€“10; [preprocessing](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py); [experiments](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/experiments.py), commit `9116ec99bf9f`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 5. Â· LILA and a healthy pressure reference\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task and preparation.** LILA identifies leaks from pairwise pressure relations and flow-related terms, then localizes candidate pipes with a calibrated hydraulic model. Reference periods and suitable sensor combinations are selected to separate overlapping leaks. Current pressure is estimated from measurements belonging to the same hydraulic condition; the target is not pressure at t+15.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Model and contribution.** Linear regression generates relative-error trajectories; CUSUM identifies changes and a most-affected sensor. The localization stage uses pressure-drop signatures and optimization, retaining up to 30 candidate pipes and a limited set of sensors near the most-affected sensor. The expanded work adds automated change-point detection and improves the localization recipe over the 2020 Leakbusters submission. Historical contest performance and expanded-paper performance are distinct versions.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluation must be read literally.** The expanded paper evaluates the 19 leaks starting in 2019 and excludes four leaks carried over from 2018. At a 300 m location tolerance and detection allowed during the leak lifespan, Table 1 reports TP=17, FP=2, FN=0, recall=1.000, precision=0.895, F1=0.944 and EUR307,852. All 19 leaks were identified; two localizations outside the permitted radius were counted as FP. Consequently FN=0 and recall=1 do not mean that all 19 passed the location criterion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Timing and foresight.** The claim of locating every event within 374 m requires the paper's wider sensitivity setting: a maximum allowed detection delay of 41 days. It is not a rapid-detection guarantee. Most abrupt leaks are detected much sooner, but p280 takes approximately 116 hours; incipient events have around 20-day mean CUSUM delay in the reported analysis. Expert annotations were made ex post, and the paper acknowledges their perfect-foresight advantage. A blind prospective procedure must not inherit those annotations as operational inputs.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Code reading.** The selected `leak_analysis` implementation includes current reference pressure and raw `PUMP_1` flow in LinearRegression; the published formulation includes squared flow terms. The exact feature recipe therefore needs version-specific checking. Perfect-team trajectories and event-specific reference windows serve retrospective analysis; they are not legitimate future-pressure predictors merely because the truth is now public.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Relation to our target.** LILA is an accessible regression/CUSUM precedent and a strong illustration of why healthy-reference estimation differs from observed-pressure forecasting. Sensor selection, overlapping-leak handling and hydraulic context already exist; our contribution cannot claim to introduce those ideas in general.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Paper](https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001535), PDF pp.4 and 7â€“9; [selected implementation](https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA/blob/b488a60db1974b6f5b7887ce1cf5d31dff3dad68/_utils/BattLeDIM_utils.py); [localization repository](https://github.com/jorgeps86/LeakLocalization). LILA snapshot commit `b488a60db197`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 6. Zhang and Fink Â· AIGNN\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task and architecture.** A neural algorithmic reasoner is pretrained on Fordâ€“Fulkerson max-flow trajectories from 1,000 random 16-node graphs. Its processor is transferred into pressure reconstruction/prediction with new encoders/decoders. ChebNetIN and ChebNetEMB inject learned embeddings as additional inputs or internal features. Algorithm-informed embeddings do not by themselves constitute a proof of exact hydraulic conservation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Data and alignment.** The nominal hydraulic model generates pressure-training labels. A reconstructor uses sparse pressure at t; a predictor uses tâˆ’T through tâˆ’1 to estimate t. The next-step formulation is therefore another direct temporal antecedent. SCADA 2018 determines diagnostic thresholds and 2019 evaluates detection/localization; sensor-relocation experiments assess generalization.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Results and version control.** In the local 2024 preprint, Table 3 reports overall relative…1983 tokens truncated…ey establish external methodological precedents but are not L-Town results. DiTEC generated datasets and EPyT-Flow are infrastructure; GraphConvWat is a reconstruction antecedent. Availability of their repositories is not proof of a reproduced result on our task.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 11. What can be claimed after this comparison\\r\\n\\r\\nPressure prediction, temporal history, topology, sparse sensors, adaptation, ensembles and robustness all have precedents. The reviewed corpus has not established a homogeneous solution/evaluation for our exact **observed-pressure +15-minute official-SCADA task, equal information budgets, strong causal baselines and separately controlled reading age**. This is a gap in the reviewed corpus, not proof of worldwide novelty.\\r\\n\\r\\nOur contribution still needs a specified rule and experimental evidence. Every comparison must align target, horizon, sensor coverage, data origin, units, chronological boundaries, tuning budget and availability. Leak truth may annotate evaluation regimes, but must not select test-based hyperparameters or enter operational forecast inputs. These conclusions preserve the Milestone 1 objective while recognizing what the literature already contributes.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe official-label audit also found active leaks at every 2019 timestamp. A global â€œany leak/no leakâ€ classifier would therefore lack negative examples in that test. This does not mean that individual pipe labels lack negatives: pipe classification, new-event detection and observed-pressure regression are different targets.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 12. Traceability and limitations\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [full state of the art](#/miniproject/milestone-2/estado-del-arte), [46-file matrix](#/miniproject/milestone-2/matriz), [repository index](#/miniproject/milestone-2/repositorios) and [static code review](#/miniproject/milestone-2/codigo-y-curso) contain the supporting versions, page references and code evidence. This chapter prioritizes task-relevant detail; it does not turn every snapshot into a complete executable clone or every result into a verified reproduction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe report uses a shorter synthesis across its two literature pages. This companion remains the detailed research repository, so the five-page limit does not force the evidence and qualifications to disappear.\\r\\n\\r\\nName                          \\r\\n---- 2.1 Topological Graph Construction
We start with modeling a WDN (topological objects of
junctions connected by pipe segments) using a graph, as
shown in Fig. 2.
Given an undirected graph G = (V, E), V is a set of nodes
where each v âˆˆV denotes a node on the graph (junctions
of the WDN) and we consider N nodes (i.e., |V| = N);

is characterised by a certain direction of flow, calling
for directed graphs; instead in this paper we consider
an undirected graph. Using undirected graphs was shown
to yield satisfactory results for pressure reconstruction
in HajgatÃ³ et al. (2021) and will thus be adapted here
as it has more applicability for real-life situations. As
novel contribution in the graph construction, we introduce
self-loops for the node sensors. This increases the node
degree of pressure sensor nodes in the WDN, amplifying
the influence of the sensor nodes during reconstruction.
Experimentally, higher node degrees graph yield to lower
detection errors than graphs without self-loops.
While G captures the topological structure of the network,
the relation of a given junction to its neighbours is de-
scribed by the adjacency matrix, A. The latter reveals
the connectivity between the nodes of a graph, which can
reflect multiple factors, such as the length of the pipe
separating them, its diameter, the pipe coefficient and
the direction of flow. Three primal weight assignments are
investigated, namely, i) unweighted edges, ii) pipe length-
based weights and variants thereof, and iii) hydraulic loss-
based weights for the pipe segments.
Unweighted edges is the most elementary approach, which
assumes binary weight value assignment as Avu

Finally, a hydraulic loss weighted graph assumes the edge

Hence, reconstructing and predicting the pressure mea-
surements in the WDN is cast as a problem of graph signal
processing. Given a partly observed WDN, its hydraulic
state is estimated at every intersection of the network by
processing the physical time-series signals in conjunction
with topological information about the piping system. This
effectively describes a graph-signal reconstruction problem
and can be addressed with the application of GNNs. In
the following section, we first introduce the field of GNNs,
motivating our architectural choices. We then conclude the
section with the proposed methodology.
2.2 Graph Signal Reconstruction and Prediction
Graph Neural Networks
GNNs are neural network archi-
tectures that can process geometrical data (non-Euclidean)
with the goal of extracting node features combined with
graph topology to solve tasks such as prediction, forecast-
ing and classification (Ortega et al. (2018)). In the litera-
ture, their architectures can be classified into spatial- and
spectral-based GNNs. The former, implement a message-
passing process along the graph at each graph-convolution
operation, which corresponds to the multiplication of a
convolution kernel with the corresponding node feature
vectors, followed by a sum or a mean rule. These method-
ologies are very simple and adapt to different network
topologies, however they suffer from a well known over-
smoothing problem. In the case of deep networks with
many hidden layers, the extracted features assimilate
across the graph. The latter, spectral-based architectures,
build on multiple hidden layers, each one performing
spectral convolutions, defined from a GSP point of view.
GSP provides a notion of frequency and a graph Fourier
transform, allowing for filtering operation in the spectral
(frequency) domain. As a consequence, a graph convolu-
tional layer, can be written by a sum of filtered signals
followed by an activation function, where each filter is
defined in the graph spectral domain (using an eigen-
decomposition of a graph Laplacian). These networks alle-
viate the oversmoothing effect, while suffering from a large
computational burden induced by the forward/inverse
graph Fourier transform. To simplify the computational
complexity, some approaches based on parameterisation
using Chebyshev polynomials Defferrard et al. (2017) have
been proposed, known as the ChebNet model.
Our Architecture
For a very sparsely monitored WDN,
as the one in our work, a deeper network, consisting of
more hidden layers, might be needed. At the same time,
to ensure a localisation of the leakage, we need to preserve
as much as local information as possible. Oversmoothing
would not allow us to preserve high-frequency (hence local)
information, thus we adopt spectral-based architectures.
Our initial ChebNet model is based on the one provided
by HajgatÃ³ et al. (2021) for the WDN of Richmond, which

=== PDF PAGE 4 ===
664
GarÃ°ar Ã–rn GarÃ°arsson et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661â€“666
Fig. 3. ChebNet model for graph signal reconstruction.
The predictor (Fig. 1) has the same structure but the
input channel chin, has a depth of 3 time periods.
has a similar number of junctions and pipes as the WDN
we benchmark our model on. The model consists solely of
Chebyshev convolutional layers, which are regularised by
the weight decay of the gradient descent optimiser. Layer
weights are initialised by the Xavier normal distribution
(Glorot and Bengio (2010)) and biases set to zero. The
hidden layers are activated with a SiLU sigmoid linear
unit (Hendrycks and Gimpel (2016)) and the output layer
is passed through a sigmoid activation. The ADAM opti-
miser (Kingma and Ba (2015)) is used due to its properties
of having an adaptive learning rate with momentum, that
adjusts to the function to be optimised.
An exhaustive ablation study to optimise hyper parame-
ters was provided by HajgatÃ³ et al. (2021) leading to the
following architecture for the WDN of Richmond: 4 Cheby-
shev spectral graph convolutional layers with the degree of
the polynomial in the hidden layers set to [K1, K2, K3, ] =
[240, 120, 20], and filter sizes [F1, F2, F3] = [120, 60, 30].
This forms the basis of our architecture, depicted in Fig. 3.
The input channel size, chin, here refers to the depth of
the input; in the case of the reconstructor, this will then
equal one, as a single input graph is used to generate the
output, whereas for the predictor, this will be equivalent
to the T number of time step used to generate the out-
put prediction. From this basis, we optimised the graph
construction methodologies (weights assignment, signal
scaling methodologies, and with or without self-loop for
observed nodes) as motivated in Sec. 2.1, and the window
size for the predictor.
Detection and Localisation Methodology
We now for-
malise the proposed method with Algorithm 1 and focus
on i) the estimation error, ii) the conversion from node
to edge error (or residual), iii) fault detection and leakage
localisation.
Node Estimation Error Evaluation
The outputs of the

generated at t âˆ’1 using past information. The difference
is the prediction error or residual signal, namely r(t) =
[r1(t), r2(t), . . . , rN(t)] = Ë†y(t)

of random noise. When this residual does not display a
stationary behavior anymore, this implies a change in the
underlying model describing the behaviour of the WDN.
In this paper, we identify this change as the presence of a
leakage.

Empirically, we use the mean square error (MSE) as a default loss function L for GATRes because it yields the
best result in our tests. In addition, inspired by BERT (Devlin et al., 2018), the loss is computed on masked
positions. After computation, model weights Î˜ are updated by the partial derivatives w.r.t the computed loss. For
detail, we refer to gradient descent optimization techniques (Kingma & Ba, 2014; Ruder, 2016). The training
progress is then iterated with different masked features ÌƒX derived from the original features X until the model
convergence.
4.4.2. Testing Details
In testing, the test graphs can be represented as Gtest = ( ÌƒXtest,Etest,Atest) . By default, topology and edge attributes
are retained as in training while ÌƒXtest is varied and its data distribution is undetermined. For the generalization
problem, Gtest can differ from the training graphs in any property. In other words, the model should be able to
estimate pressure on an unseen topology and an unknown data distribution.
When the temporal dimension is involved, the test graph at a particular time t is denoted as Gt
test. As the designed
model takes only one snapshot as input, we feed temporal Gt
test into the trained GNN model sequentially and
individually. In other words, previously inferred outcomes do not impact any reconstruction result within a testing
scenario. These scenarios are considered realâ€world situations associated with inherent uncertainty due to dy-
namic factors (Zhou et al., 2023). To reflect this uncertainty in our testing, we adopt a distortion method on
junction demands during the testing phase, drawing inspiration from (MÃ¼cke et al., 2023; Zhou et al., 2023).
Specifically, we outline two strategies as follows:
Clean test. We consider an assumption of no uncertainty, ensuring that baseline models observe clear,
precisely calibrated pressure. As these models treat each snapshot as an independent sample, we gauge
the model adaptability across different sensor configurations using a distinct mask for each snapshot in
every trial. The statistical outcomes from 100 trials, encompassing diverse metered locations, illustrate
the model performance under typical conditions.
Noisy test. Following (Zhou et al., 2023), we inject Gaussian noise into junction demands before processing
the simulation and then pair each outcome snapshot with a random mask for a test case. Pipe roughness,
another considered parameter, is intentionally excluded due to its inconsistency across different headloss
formulas used for each water network. Notably, the Hazenâ€Williams and Darcyâ€Weisbach formulas are
sensitive to pipe roughness, whereas the Chezyâ€Manning formula remains unaffected by this parameter
(Klise et al., 2018). In the interest of generalization, we have opted to exclude this parameter. Never-
theless, we set a tougher noise for junction demands beyond the original tests. The new noisy test involves
the mean and standard deviation of 10% and 100% of the initial demands, respectively. We run 100 test
cases and report statistical findings.
5. Experiment Settings
5.1. Data Sets
The main use case in this study was performed using a private largeâ€scale WDN in The Netherlands in the area of
Oosterbeek. The network comprises 5,855 junctions and 6,188 pipes. Figure 5a shows the topology and the
pressures at the nodes from some random snapshot of Oosterbeek WDN.
We also used four publicly available WDNs benchmarks, namely Anytown (Walski et al., 1987), Câ€Town
(Ostfeld et al., 2012), Lâ€Town (Vrachimis et al., 2022), and Richmond (Van Zyl, 2001) to provide a baseline
for evaluation and reproducibility of our work. Finally, in the experiments related to model generalization, we
used two additional public data sets, Ky13 (Hernadez et al., 2016) and an anonymized WDN called â€œLargeâ€
(Sitzenfrei et al., 2023). The WDNs used in this study vary in size and structure, ranging from small and medium
size to largeâ€scale networks like â€œLargeâ€ and Oosterbeek, as can be seen in Figure 5. Table 2 shows the main
characteristics of each network.
5.2. Baseline Models Settings
Generally, two goals dictate the baseline selection. Section 3 mentions the first goal: to try out popular GNN
architectures on a nodeâ€level regression problem. The aim is to achieve acceptable errors when the models are

tested on data points that are from an unknown â€œnatureâ€ distribution. The second goal is to explore existing
frameworks, from synthesized data querying and model training to evaluation phases. We aim to establish a
reliable benchmarking framework for pressure estimation tasks or problems related to WDNs. In other words, the
model that performs better in our tests should be more useful in practical applications.
For this purpose, we compare our GATRes architecture to popular GNNs, including GCNii (M. Chen et al., 2020)
and GAT (VeliÄkoviÄ‡ et al., 2018). In addition to this, GraphConWat (GCW) (HajgatÃ³ et al., 2021a, 2021b) and
mGCN (Ashraf et al., 2023), which are dominant approaches in solving pressure estimation using GNN with
sparse information, are also considered in our comparison. Table 3 summarizes the model settings.
GATResâ€small with hyperparameters is the optimal version after the optimization process, which will be carefully
explained in the latter section. To study the impact of the model size, we also introduce GATResâ€large scaling
close to mGCN in terms of the number of parameters.
GAT remained at a shallow depth to prevent the oversmoothing problem (D. Chen et al., 2020). Precisely,
neighbor features encoded by a tooâ€deep GNN converged to indistinguishable embeddings that harm the model
performance. Empirically, we balance the tradeâ€off between performance and efficiency for each model to select
the appropriate hyperparameters.
In GraphConvWat models, we detached binary masks from the input features as these masks did not improve the
model performance, which aligns with the findings in Ashraf et al. (2023). Furthermore, GraphConvWat tuned is
a lightweight version in which the degrees of the Chebyshev polynomial Ki are set to smaller values to reduce
complexity and work surprisingly well in our experiments.
Training mGCN slightly diverged from its original work in sensor positions. Concretely (Ashraf et al., 2023),
trained mGCN using fixed sensors with extensive historical data. However, as we explained in Section 4.4, this
data was inaccessible throughout training. Therefore, we fed different random masks into the model in each
epoch. Considering a synthetic data set, the model had an opportunity to capture meaningful patterns in various
sensor positions. Additionally, mGCN incorporated static edge attributes such as pipe length and diameter in its
final decisionâ€making process.
We then provide an overview of other hyperparameters, applicable to any mentioned model. Each model was
trained in a fixed number of iterations, soâ€called epochs, throughout the entire training set. In addition, the whole

5
Experiments
We empirically evaluate our proposed method in an online scenario for detecting
leakages and sensor faults in water distribution networks â€“ all experiments are
implemented in Python1.
5.1
Data
We use a version of the L-Town water distribution network as used in [16], as a
prominent realistic benchmark for anomaly detection â€“ we only use Area A which
consists of 661 nodes, 766 links, and 29 (optimally placed) pressure sensors. We
build and simulate 10 scenarios where the first 5 scenarios each contain a single
leakage â€“ we vary position, time and size of the leakage â€“ and the remaining 5
scenarios each contain a single diï¬€erent sensor fault (position is varied):
â€“ Scenario 6: Sensor measurement is overflowing over time â€“ i.e. it is going to
infinity over time.
â€“ Scenario 7: Gaussian noise is added to the sensor measurement.
â€“ Scenario 8: A constant value is added to the sensor measurement.
â€“ Scenario 9: Sensor measurement is set equal to zero.
â€“ Scenario 10: Sensor measurement is shifted by a small amount.
Each scenario is simulated (using WNTR [8]) for 3 months and pressure sensors
are sampled every 5 min.
1 Implementation is available on GitHub: https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-
kNN-Regressor OnlineLearning WDNs.

=== PDF PAGE 9 ===
SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks
759
Fig. 1. L-Town network [16] â€“ we only use â€œArea Aâ€ where we have 29 pressure sensors.
The data stream of sensor measurements is post processed by using a sliding
window of size 4 â€“ we average all samples dimensional wise in this time window,
so that we end up with 29 dimensional samples.
5.2
Setup
We compare our proposed SAM-kNN regressor (see Sect. 4) to several other
online learning regressors. In order to justify the introduced overhead of the SAM
architecture, we compare its performance to vanilla regressors (kNN regression
and linear regression) wrapped as online learners by using the river toolbox2.
For each pressure sensor, we build a corresponding virtual sensor based on
all other pressure sensors â€“ i.e. we try to predict (using a regressor) the pressure
based on the past pressure values of all other pressure sensors (see Sect. 3).
These virtual sensors are then used for a residual based anomaly detection â€“
i.e. an alarm (detected anomaly) is raised when the predicted pressure value
deviates too much from the observed pressure measurement. For each scenario,
the processed data stream is fed as batches of 200 samples to the regressors
(realizing the virtual sensors).
For each regressor, we evaluate the performance of the resulting anomaly
detector â€“ since we are interested in the detection of a single anomaly (leakage
and sensor fault), we report true positives (TP), false positives (FP) and false
negatives (FN). Note that the true positives and false negatives are always either
0 or 1 because we only check whether an alarm was raised when the single
anomaly was present or not â€“ however, for the false positives, we count every
single false alarm.
2 https://github.com/online-ml/river.

760
J. Jakob et al.
5.3
Results
The results for leakage detection, for each regressor and each scenario, are shown
in Table 1. Likewise, the results for sensor fault detection are shown in Table 2.
We observe the same/similar eï¬€ects for both types of anomalies (leakages and
sensor faults): We observe that linear regression completely fails to detect the
anomaly â€“ this indicates that a linear model is not suï¬ƒcient to model the
hydraulic dynamics in the water distribution network and hence fails to detect
any anomalies. There is only one exception: For scenario 6 the linear model is
able to detect the sensor fault â€“ recall that in this particular scenario the sen-
sor fault is characterized by a slowly overflowing sensor measurements (i.e. the
pressure value goes to infinity), which is expected to be easily detected because
it is a very â€œloudâ€ fault. The kNN model shows good performance in detecting
the anomaly but has a huge number of false positives â€“ i.e. it is too sensitive and
raises lots of false alarms. Our proposed SAM-kNN shows the best performance
â€“ it is able to consistently detect the anomalies while having a small number
of false positives only. The huge reduction of the false positives in comparison

For many years, mathematical simulation tools have been the most common approach to reconstructing an estimate of the WDNs hydraulics. However, pure physics-based simulations involve several challenges, for example, partially observable data, high uncertainty, and extensive manual calibration.\\nL192: Thus, data-driven approaches have gained traction to overcome such limitations. In this work, we combine physics-based modeling and graph neural networks (GNN), a data-driven approach, to address the pressure estimation problem. Our work has two main contributions. First, a training strategy that relies on random sensor placement making our GNN-based estimation model robust to unexpected sensor location changes.\\nL193: Second, a realistic evaluation protocol that considers real temporal patterns and noise injection to mimic the uncertainties intrinsic to real-world scenarios. As a result, a new state-of-the-art model, GAT with Residual Connections, for pressure estimation is available. Our model surpasses the performance of previous studies on several WDNs benchmarks, showing a reduction of absolute error of ≈40% on average.\\nL194: ## Plain Language Summary\\nL195: Water management practitioners have resorted to mathematical simulation tools to reconstruct pressure, flow, and demand in order to improve their control operations. However, pure physics-based methods need to deal with partially observable data, high uncertainty, and extensive manual calibration. We combine physics-based modeling and graph neural networks, a data-driven approach, to address the pressure estimation problem and overcome those limitations. Our work has two main contributions.\\nL196: First, a random sensor placement strategy makes our estimation model resilient to unexpected sensor location changes. Second, a realistic evaluation protocol that considers real temporal patterns and noise injection to mimic the uncertainties of real-world scenarios. As a result, a new state-of-the-art model, GAT with Residual Connections, for pressure estimation is available.\\nL197: Our model surpasses the performance of previous studies on several water distribution networks benchmarks, showing a reduction of absolute error of ≈40% on average.\\nL198: ## 1 Introduction\\nL199: State Estimation in water distribution networks (WDNs) is a general problem that encompasses pressure and flow estimation, often using scarce and sparsely located sensor devices. WDNs management companies rely on such estimations for optimizing their operations. Knowing the state of the network at any given time enables water managers to perform real-time monitoring and control operations.\\nL200: The research community and practitioners working in this field have resorted for many years to the power of mathematical simulation tools to reconstruct an estimate of the system hydraulics (Arsene & Gabrys, cite33†2014 ; Kang & Lansey, cite34†2009 ; Koşucu et al., cite35†2022 ; Menapace et al., cite36†2018 ; Ruiz et al., cite37†2022 ; Todini et al., cite38†2021 ).\\nL201: However, pure physics-based simulation approaches have to overcome the challenges of (a) data scarcity which translates to partially observable systems, (b) high uncertainty introduced by the large number of parameters to configure, unexpected changes in consumers' behavior reflected in uncertain demand patterns, and noisy sensor measurements, and (c) extensive manual configuration for model calibration using metered data, which requires expert knowledge and usually hinders model re-usability in a different WDN (Ostfeld et al., cite39†2012 ; Wang et al., cite40†2021 ).\\nL202: The challenges associated with physics-based modeling of WDNs have motivated researchers to investigate the usage of data-driven approaches, or a combination of both, to address the state estimation problem (Lima et al., cite41†2018 ; Meirelles et al., cite42†2017 ).\\nL203: Graph neural networks (GNNs) is a data-driven approach that has shown successful results in several estimation problems where data can be modeled as a graph. Since WDNs can be naturally modeled as a graph, GNNs can exploit the relational inductive biases imposed by the graph topology. As a result, GNNs have also attracted the attention of researchers in the field of WDNs.\\nL204: For example, Tsiami and Makropoulos (cite43†2021 ) used temporal graph Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), a combination of CNNs and GNNs, to extract temporal and spatial features simultaneously in a model to detect cyber-physical attacks in WDNs. Zanfei, Menapace, et al. (cite44†2022 ) used GNNs for implementing burst detection algorithms. GNNs are also used for integrated water network partitioning and dynamic district metered areas (Fu et al., cite45†2022 ).\\nL205: In the context of Digital Twins of WDNs, a GNN-based model is used for Pump Speed-Based State Estimation (Bonilla et al., cite46†2022 ). Water demand forecasting has been also addressed using GNNs (Zanfei, Brentan, et al., cite47†2022 ). Metamodeling is another interesting area where GNNs have been leveraged for the estimation of pressures and flows (Kerimov et al., cite48†2023 ; Lima et al., cite41†2018 ; Zanfei et al., cite49†2023 ). GNN-based models has been also used to solve problems related to water quality.\\nL206: For example, Z. Li et al. (cite50†2024a ) propose GNNs for identifying contamination sources in water distribution systems, and for water quality prediction (Z. Li et al., cite51†2024b ). Other recent works on GNNs for pressure estimation are (Ashraf et al., cite52†2023 ; Hajgató et al., cite53†2021a , cite54†2021b ). The main differences of those approaches with ours are the training strategy and the evaluation protocol to assess the model performance.\\nL207: Our proposed techniques in this regard are more realistic and give the model the ability to adapt to unexpected changes.\\nL208: In this work, we focus on pressure estimation by leveraging both physics-based simulation models and GNN-based data-driven approaches. We rely on a data generation method that leverages the EPANET simulation tool to overcome the lack of data required for model training. However, in our approach we include all dynamic parameters (e.g., reservoir total heads, tank levels, roughness coefficient) which were not considered in previous works.\\nL209: This contributes to data variety and avoids that uncertainties propagate due to model simplification errors (Du et al., cite55†2018 ). Our main contributions are twofold. First, our GNN-based estimation model is robust to unexpected sensor's location changes due to the proposed training strategy that relies on random sensor placement. Second, our evaluation protocol considers real time-dependent patterns and additionally injects the uncertainties intrinsic to real-world scenarios.\\nL210: The outcome is a new state-of-the-art GNN-based model, GAT with Residual Connections (GATRes), for pressure estimation in WDNs.\\nL211: GATRes is able to reconstruct the junction pressures of Oosterbeek, a large-scale WDN in the Netherlands, with an average 1.94 m absolute error, which represents an 8.57% improvement with respect to other models. Similarly, our model outperformed previous approaches on other WDNs benchmark data sets.\\nL212: The highest improvement was seen for C-Town WDN (Ostfeld et al., cite39†2012 ) with an absolute error decrease of 52.36%, for Richmond (Van Zyl, cite56†2001 ) an error decrease of 5.31%, and 40.35% error decrease for L-Town (Vrachimis et al., cite57†2022 ). In addition, our first attempt on model generalization shows that a multi-graph pre-training followed by fine-tuning helps to increase the model performance. The absolute error on Oosterbeek network was reduced by ≈2% following our generalization strategy.\\nL213: The remainder of this document is as follows. Section cite58†2 presents the problem statement, describes the issues that need to be addressed by pressure reconstruction models and defines the criteria to assess the model capabilities. Section cite59†3 depicts the related work in the field, narrowed to GNNs for node-level regression tasks and how previous work on GNN-based pressure estimation satisfies the criteria defined in the Section cite58†2 .\\nL214: The methodology is presented in Section cite60†4 , including the data generation process, a detailed description of our model architecture, and the details of the proposed approach for model training and evaluation. Section cite61†5 describes the setup of the experimental phase. It includes a description of WDNs benchmark data sets used in this work, the base model configurations, and the evaluation metrics. Section cite62†6 describes all the empirical evaluations of our approach.\\nL215: First, the experiments on the main use case of this study, Oosterbeek WDN, are depicted. Then, the experiments toward model generalization are shown. Next, the performance of the proposed model on different benchmark WDNs is presented. This section concludes with an ablation study to identify the contribution of the different components of the model architecture. A discussion of the most salient findings are presented in Section cite63†7 . Finally, the conclusions are presented in Section cite64†8 .\\nL216: ## 2 Pressure Estimation in Water Distribution Networks\\nL217: ### 2.1 Problem Statement\\nL218: Hydraulic experts have managed WDNs using essential measurements such as flow, demand, and pressure. These measurements offer a comprehensive perspective of a WDN, forming a foundation for various supervisory tasks like forecasting (Iwakin & Moazeni, cite65†2024 ), leak detection (Garðarsson et al., cite66†2022 ), and operational control (Nerantzis et al., cite67†2020 ).\\nL219: For this reason, this study focuses on addressing the fundamental challenge of approximating measurements across all nodal locations within water networks. In this investigation, we initially assume that the prior knowledge (i.e., historical data) is unavailable and network states are discretely recorded under typical conditions, disregarding unforeseen events like leaks, earthquakes, or fires.\\nL220: Additionally, we presume that measured states between two neighborhoods within the network exhibit a degree of similarity. These assumptions are crucial for employing a data-driven machine learning model trained from valid cases while avoiding corruption in the complexities of water networks.\\nL221: A real-life WDN consists of diverse components such as tanks, valves, pumps, reservoirs, and thousands of customer junctions whose measurement states are crucial for management. In this study, we narrow the scope and favor pressure as the primary measurement due to the ease of meter installation and the more affordable price compared to flow ones (Zhou et al., cite68†2019 ). Nevertheless, these pressure sensors are limited in practice due to infrastructural limits and privacy concerns.\\nL222: Consequently, the gathered data, known as the pressure states of the junction nodes, are scarce. These states play crucial roles as samples in training data-driven approaches aligned with a machine-learning model that often requires a large amount of data. As a prerequisite, pressure states should be fully observable to minimize the estimation loss of the trained model in all customer positions throughout the water network.\\nL223: This contradiction poses a challenge in applying the machine-learning approach to solve pressure estimation tasks.\\nL224: The application context is about what and when the trained model should be applied. Generally, a model is often associated with a unique water network and fixed sensors previously seen during training. Also, the training environment may exclude noisy, uncertain conditions that could affect the model's decision-making. In other words, these challenges result in worse model performance when faced with unfamiliar network topologies or uncertain situations.\\nL225: Consequently, model retraining is inevitable, albeit such training is an expensive and unsustainable approach. This concern enhances the necessity of the generalization ability of pressure estimation models, which needs to be addressed in prior research. Before addressing this research gap, we will first delve into the specific problem within water networks and lay out the criteria necessary for a robust pressure estimation model.\\nL226: ### 2.2 Partially-Observable Dat…5980 tokens truncated…moid linear\\r\\nunit (Hendrycks and Gimpel (2016)) and the output layer\\r\\nis passed through a sigmoid activation. The ADAM opti-\\r\\nmiser (Kingma and Ba (2015)) is used due to its properties\\r\\nof having an adaptive learning rate with momentum, that\\r\\nadjusts to the function to be optimised.\\r\\nAn exhaustive ablation study to optimise hyper parame-\\r\\nters was provided by HajgatÃ³ et al. (2021) leading to the\\r\\nfollowing architecture for the WDN of Richmond: 4 Cheby-\\r\\nshev spectral graph convolutional layers with the degree of\\r\\nthe polynomial in the hidden layers set to [K1, K2, K3, ] =\\r\\n[240, 120, 20], and filter sizes [F1, F2, F3] = [120, 60, 30].\\r\\nThis forms the basis of our architecture, depicted in Fig. 3.\\r\\nThe input channel size, chin, here refers to the depth of\\r\\nthe input; in the case of the reconstructor, this will then\\r\\nequal one, as a single input graph is used to generate the\\r\\noutput, whereas for the predictor, this will be equivalent\\r\\nto the T number of time step used to generate the out-\\r\\nput prediction. From this basis, we optimised the graph\\r\\nconstruction methodologies (weights assignment, signal\\r\\nscaling methodologies, and with or without self-loop for\\r\\nobserved nodes) as motivated in Sec. 2.1, and the window\\r\\nsize for the predictor.\\r\\nDetection and Localisation Methodology\\r\\nWe now for-\\r\\nmalise the proposed method with Algorithm 1 and focus\\r\\non i) the estimation error, ii) the conversion from node\\r\\nto edge error (or residual), iii) fault detection and leakage\\r\\nlocalisation.\\r\\nNode Estimation Error Evaluation\\r\\nThe outputs of the\\r\\npredictor and reconstructor are compared, by evaluating\\r\\nat each iteration the difference between the reconstructed\\r\\npressure at time t with the predicted one, which was\\r\\ngenerated at t âˆ’1 using past information. The difference\\r\\nis the prediction error or residual signal, namely r(t) =\\r\\n[r1(t), r2(t), . . . , rN(t)] = Ë†y(t)\\r\\np\\r\\nâˆ’Ë†y(t)\\r\\nr . Under nominal non-\\r\\nfaulty conditions, the residual error should have stationary\\r\\nbehavior and should be caused mainly by the presence\\r\\nof random noise. When this residual does not display a\\r\\nstationary behavior anymore, this implies a change in the\\r\\nunderlying model describing the behaviour of the WDN.\\r\\nIn this paper, we identify this change as the presence of a\\r\\nleakage.\\r\\nAlgorithm 1 Leak detection pseudocode\\r\\n1: for every_timestep : t do\\r\\n2:\\r\\nreadings â†readPressureSensors()\\r\\n3:\\r\\nif first_run then\\r\\n4:\\r\\nnext_prediction â†GNN_1.predict(readings)\\r\\n5:\\r\\nelse\\r\\n6:\\r\\n// Calculate prediction error\\r\\n7:\\r\\nlast_prediction â†next_prediction\\r\\n8:\\r\\nreconstruction â†GNN_2.predict(readings)\\r\\n9:\\r\\nÎ˜\\r\\nL(Xâ€²,X)\\r\\n(7)\\r\\nFigure 4. Graph neural network (GNN) training scheme. Given clean snapshots, we mask out a significant number (95%)\\r\\nof node features. The remaining data is then sent into a GNN playing as an autoencoder to rebuild missing values with regard\\r\\nto graph properties (such as topology and edge attributes). GNN weights are updated using the loss derived by the predicted\\r\\nand ground truth values at masked places.\\r\\nWater Resources Research\\r\\n10.1029/2023WR036741\\r\\nTRUONG ET AL.\\r\\n11 of 27\\r\\n19447973, 2024, 7, Downloaded from https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1029/2023WR036741 by Uva Universiteitsbibliotheek, Wiley Online Library on [10/07/2024]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 12 ===\\r\\nEmpirically, we use the mean square error (MSE) as a default loss function L for GATRes because it yields the\\r\\nbest result in our tests. In addition, inspired by BERT (Devlin et al., 2018), the loss is computed on masked\\r\\npositions. After computation, model weights Î˜ are updated by the partial derivatives w.r.t the computed loss. For\\r\\ndetail, we refer to gradient descent optimization techniques (Kingma & Ba, 2014; Ruder, 2016). The training\\r\\nprogress is then iterated with different masked features ÌƒX derived from the original features X until the model\\r\\nconvergence.\\r\\n4.4.2. Testing Details\\r\\nIn testing, the test graphs can be represented as Gtest = ( ÌƒXtest,Etest,Atest) . By default, topology and edge attributes\\r\\nare retained as in training while ÌƒXtest is varied and its data distribution is undetermined. For the generalization\\r\\nproblem, Gtest can differ from the training graphs in any property. In other words, the model should be able to\\r\\nestimate pressure on an unseen topology and an unknown data distribution.\\r\\nWhen the temporal dimension is involved, the test graph at a particular time t is denoted as Gt\\r\\ntest. As the designed\\r\\nmodel takes only one snapshot as input, we feed temporal Gt\\r\\ntest into the trained GNN model sequentially and\\r\\nindividually. In other words, previously inferred outcomes do not impact any reconstruction result within a testing\\r\\nscenario. These scenarios are considered realâ€world situations associated with inherent uncertainty due to dy-\\r\\nnamic factors (Zhou et al., 2023). To reflect this uncertainty in our testing, we adopt a distortion method on\\r\\njunction demands during the testing phase, drawing inspiration from (MÃ¼cke et al., 2023; Zhou et al., 2023).\\r\\nSpecifically, we outline two strategies as follows:\\r\\nClean test. We consider an assumption of no uncertainty, ensuring that baseline models observe clear,\\r\\nprecisely calibrated pressure. As these models treat each snapshot as an independent sample, we gauge\\r\\nthe model adaptability across different sensor configurations using a distinct mask for each snapshot in\\r\\nevery trial. The statistical outcomes from 100 trials, encompassing diverse metered locations, illustrate\\r\\nthe model performance under typical conditions.\\r\\nNoisy test. Following (Zhou et al., 2023), we inject Gaussian noise into junction demands before processing\\r\\nthe simulation and then pair each outcome snapshot with a random mask for a test case. Pipe roughness,\\r\\nanother considered parameter, is intentionally excluded due to its inconsistency across different headloss\\r\\nformulas used for each water network. Notably, the Hazenâ€Williams and Darcyâ€Weisbach formulas are\\r\\nsensitive to pipe roughness, whereas the Chezyâ€Manning formula remains unaffected by this parameter\\r\\n(Klise et al., 2018). In the interest of generalization, we have opted to exclude this parameter. Never-\\r\\ntheless, we set a tougher noise for junction demands beyond the original tests. The new noisy test involves\\r\\nthe mean and standard deviation of 10% and 100% of the initial demands, respectively. We run 100 test\\r\\ncases and report statistical findings.\\r\\n5. Experiment Settings\\r\\n5.1. Data Sets\\r\\nThe main use case in this study was performed using a private largeâ€scale WDN in The Netherlands in the area of\\r\\nOosterbeek. # Computer Use

Use this skill to automate the UI of Microsoft Windows apps. It uses SendInput, UI Automation, and Windows.Graphics.Capture screenshots that work even when windows are occluded.

If this plugin is available, read this entire `SKILL.md` once before Windows automation work, before saying Computer Use is unavailable, and before falling back to other Windows automation.

Start with the directions in the Initialize section below. Read these bundled Markdown files relative to this `SKILL.md` when you need information about the specific topic they cover:

- `../../docs/guidance.md`: core runtime behavior, target-window workflow, screenshot handling, and recovery guidance. You MUST read this before controlling Windows apps.
- `../../docs/api.md`: full `sky` API reference. Read this when you need method signatures or object shapes.
- `../../docs/confirmations.md`: you MUST read this before deciding whether a Windows UI action needs confirmation

## Initialize

The bundled `cua_node` `@oai/sky` package is the core entry point for Computer Use. Import it directly by package name from the JavaScript session. Do not spawn `codex-computer-use.exe`, search for the helper executable, or build a custom helper protocol client.

Run this once per fresh `node_repl` JavaScript session:

```js
if (!globalThis.sky) {
  const { sky } = await import("@oai/sky");
  globalThis.sky = sky;
}
```
# GATRes
![GATRes architecture](model_architecture.png)
**G**raph **A**ttention Ne**T**work with **Res**idual connections

This is the official repository for the paper: **"Graph Neural Networks for Pressure Estimation in Water Distribution Systems"**.

## UPDATE: (25/03/2025)
We refactorize the structure to use this library as a separable python package.

By default, all generation flags are set to False. This allows user to choose which parameter is samplied.


First, you can create a dataset based on the default config using the below command:

``python gnn_pressure_estimation/scenegenv7.py  --executors 2 --batch_size 50 --gen_demand --gen_res_total_head --config "configs/v7.1/ctown_7v1__EPYNET_config.ini``

The command samples `nodal demand` and `reservoir total head` while keeping other values as they are in the INP file.
Note that, in very rare cases, dataset creation may fail due to stochastic nature. If this happens, try generating it again.

The created dataset, `ctown.zip`, should be located in the `datasets` folder. 

Next, we can train GATRes using the command:

``python gnn_pressure_estimation/train.py --model gatres_small --dataset_paths "datasets/ctown.zip" --input_paths "inputs/ctown.inp" --batch_size 32``


## Installation

You can follow the instructions to run the data generation tool and GATRes models. 

1. Clone or download this repository.
2. Activate *venv* environment with available Python (>=3.9), PyTorch (>=2.0), PyTorch Geometric (>=2.3), and/or CUDA (11.8). In case of a new environment, consider installing the required libraries via this command:

    ``pip install -r requirement.txt``

## Repo map

```
    |-- checkpoints                 - dir for storing model checkpoints #created in dataset generation process
    |-- configs                     - config storage for data generation tool  
    |-- experiment_logs             - dir for tracking experiment logs #created for loading model weights
    |-- generator                   - code for data generation tool
    |-- inputs                      - water distribution network topologies
    |-- utils                       - auxiliary functions for running models
    |-- ConfigModels.py             - default model configurations for running models
    |-- GraphModels.py              - model definition
    |-- evaluation.py               - code for testing model
    |-- train.py                    - code for training model
```

## Data generation tool

This section will give you an instruction to generate an example dataset. A dataset is a set of arbitrary snapshots, an instantaneous water network state at a particular time. A state can consist of one (some) measurement, such as pressures, heads, demands, etc... To create snapshots, run the following steps:

1. Add a preferred input file (.INP) that contains a topology into the `inputs` folder. 
Note: Skip this step if you use default public water networks in the folder (under public licenses).

2. Add a data generation config (.INI) file. User can create a dummy one using this command:
```
python generator\EPYNET\ConfigCreator.py -r inputs\<your_inp_path>
```
You can find the created config file (.INI) in the `configs` folder.

3. Run this command to create a dataset:
```
python generator\EPYNET\scenegenv7.py   --config <your_ini_path>
                                        --executors <num_of_workers>
                                        --batch_size <batch_size_per_worker>
```
There are many flags and options introduced in `scenegenv7.py`.

The new dataset will be a zip file (.ZIP).


## Training model

User can train GATRes model using this default command:
```
python train.py --model gatres_small
                --epochs 500
                --batch_size 8
                --device 'cuda'
                --mask_rate 0.95
                --datasets_paths <dataset_path1> <optional_dataset_path2> ...
                --input_paths <inp_path1> <optional_inp_path2> ...
                --test_data_path <test_dataset_path>
                --test_input_path <test_dataset_path>
                --save_path <where_to_save_checkpoints>
```

## Inference

When you run the above command, the trained model will be tested immediately after training. Otherwise, you can make a manual test using this command:

```
python evaluation.py    --model gatres_small
                        --model_path <where_to_load_trained_weights>
                        --batch_size 8
                        --num_test_trials 10
                        --test_type 'clean'
                        --device 'cuda'
                        --mask_rate 0.95
                        --datasets_paths <train_dataset_path> 
                        --input_paths <train_inp_path>
                        --test_data_path <test_dataset_path>
                        --test_input_path <test_dataset_path>
```

## License

=== PDF PAGE 11 ===
Conclusions
In this work, a novel framework called EILF is proposed for timely,
accurate, and effective detection and isolation of overlapping leak-
ages. Flow and pressure features are highlighted through the esti-
mation stage, multiple abrupt and incipient leakages are detected
through the identification stage, and the leak hotspots are local-
ized through an improved differential search localization stage.
The multiscale differential operation, a simple but efficient math-
ematical method, is employed to effectively extract leak-induced
mutation, reduce uncertainty, and improve localizing accuracy in
the WDSs. Two case studies, a real-life network and an L-town
network, are applied to demonstrate the proposed method. The con-
clusions are drawn as follows:
1. Applying the empirical mode decomposition and time series de-
composition, accurate estimations of flow under the normal con-
dition and pressure-drop trend are used to extract leak-induced
values from the monitoring data. Rather than analyzing original
flow and pressure signals directly, this study highlights the es-
sential leak characteristics that reduce the impact of diurnal var-
iations and provides a better solution for leak detection.
2. Multiscale difference series are calculated to reduce the nonsta-
tionary random item of residuals, the residual difference series
are used for leak identification. Similarly, the weekly difference
idea is employed to design the objective function for the locali-
zation searching problem. This approach improves the ability to
deal with uncertainty.
3. The equality of the variation of the flow residuals and the rank-
ing of pressure nodes are used to match the start time and the
end time of an abrupt leakage. Meanwhile, the sliding window-
based variation method is proposed to detect the start time and
leak size of incipient leakages. The EILF method provided an
effective multiple overlapping leak detection framework.
4. The method detects and localizes 16 leakages in 2019 with a
true positive (TPR) of 47.83%, a false positive of 5, and an eco-
nomic score of â‚¬167,981. A comparative result shows that EILF
achieved a good grade in BattleDIM 2020, demonstrating the
effectiveness of the solution for leak detection and localization
in water distribution systems. The application of this method in
real-life networks also provides a good example for rapid leak
detection in data-driven networks. The result shows that the
EILF shows good prospects for application in water distribution
systems.
Data Availability Statement
The case data and the model used in this study can be made avail-
able from BattleDIM 2020, and the codes for the proposed method
in Spyder3.7 can be made available by the corresponding author by
request.
Acknowledgments
This work was supported by the National Natural Science Founda-
of Anytown, was using data that included realistic demand patterns per node.
In the case of Câ€Town and Richmond, the WDN snapshots for evaluation
were created using a 24â€hr demand pattern time series sampled at 5 min in-
terval. Lâ€Town evaluation snapshots were created using a 1â€week demand
pattern time series sampled at 5 min interval. Table 10 shows the results of the
performance comparison of 10 runs per WDN, and then the mean and stan-
dard deviation are reported. In order to make the test deterministic we hard
coded 10 different seeds, one for each run. Then, the same seed was used for
each run, for all three models, to ensure a fair comparison of the results. As can
be seen from the table, our model GATResâ€small achieves the lowest MAE in
all WDNs and the lowest MAPE in all networks but Richmond. Likewise,
GATResâ€small achieves the higher NSE in all WDNs but Richmond.
One limitation of previous approaches is the evaluation of model performance
on unrealistic data, that is, an exact copy of the training data distribution (Section 2.2). In previous approaches, the
snapshots representing random WDN states used for training, validation and test are created by the same algo-
rithm. Consequently, the distribution of the data used for testing is a fidelity copy of the data used for training.
However, in practice, the distribution of the real data differs from the data used for training the reconstruction
models, as explained in Section 2.2. Therefore, it is important that the models adapt to circumvent such un-
certainties. Previous approaches achieve impressive performance when tested on replicas of the training data (see
Table 11), but the performance drop is evident when they are evaluated on a realistic scenario (see Table 10).
The density distributions of training and test sets in Câ€Town, Richmond, and Lâ€Town WDNs are shown in
Figure 9. It is clear that the distributions of the training and testing data created by the same algorithm (math-
ematical simulation) are identical, while the distribution of the test data with a demand pattern greatly differs from
the one used during training. This shows the ability of GATRes to adapt to the changes that occur in real life
scenarios. It also shows that other models achieve better results only when evaluated on fidelity copies of the
training data, caused by overfitting due to the large model complexity of the previous approaches. The density
distribution plot in Figure 9b explains the good performance of mGCN on Richmond WDN in terms of MAPE
and NSE (Table 10), the timeâ€based demand pattern test data set has a similar distribution than the one used for
training.

component is removed or altered, and GATRes is restored to its original version before a new change is made. The
different variants used in the ablation study are the following:
